\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\left.4\kappa_{(10)(00)}^{(\ell)}K_{(10)}^{(\ell)}\left\langle\partial^{4}(\sigma^{\prime})^{2}\right\rangle_{K^{(\ell)}}+\left(2\kappa_{(10)(10)}^{(\ell)}+4\kappa_{(11)(00)}^{(\ell)}\right)\left\langle\partial^{2}(\sigma^{\prime})^{2}\right\rangle_{K^{(\ell)}}\right\}+O(n^{-2}).

Comparing this with the recursion (
10.7
) for
K_{(11)}^{(\ell)}
shows that
S_{(11)}^{(\ell)}=O(n^{-1})
and yields the recursion

\displaystyle S_{(11)}^{(\ell+1)}

\displaystyle=\chi_{\perp}^{(\ell)}S_{(11)}^{(\ell)}+\frac{C_{W}}{2}K_{(11)}^{(\ell)}S_{(00)}^{(\ell)}\left\langle\partial^{2}(\sigma^{\prime})^{2}\right\rangle_{K^{(\ell)}}+O(n^{-2})

\displaystyle+C_{W}\left(K_{(10)}^{(\ell)}\right)^{2}\left[\left\langle\partial^{2}(\sigma^{\prime})^{2}\right\rangle_{K^{(\ell)}}+\frac{1}{2}S_{(00)}^{(\ell)}\left\langle\partial^{4}(\sigma^{\prime})^{2}\right\rangle_{K^{(\ell)}}\right]+2C_{W}K_{(10)}^{(\ell)}S_{(10)}^{(\ell)}\left\langle\partial^{2}(\sigma^{\prime})^{2}\right\rangle_{K^{(\ell)}}

\displaystyle+\frac{C_{W}}{8}\left\{\kappa_{(00)(00)}^{(\ell)}\left[K_{(11)}^{(\ell)}\left\langle\partial^{4}(\sigma^{\prime})^{2}\right\rangle_{K^{(\ell)}}+\left(K_{(10)}^{(\ell)}\right)^{2}\left\langle\partial^{6}(\sigma^{\prime})^{2}\right\rangle_{K^{(\ell)}}\right]\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\left.4\kappa_{(10)(00)}^{(\ell)}K_{(10)}^{(\ell)}\left\langle\partial^{4}(\sigma^{\prime})^{2}\right\rangle_{K^{(\ell)}}+\left(2\kappa_{(10)(10)}^{(\ell)}+4\kappa_{(11)(00)}^{(\ell)}\right)\left\langle\partial^{2}(\sigma^{\prime})^{2}\right\rangle_{K^{(\ell)}}\right\}.

Recalling the asymptotics from Lemma
11.1
and Proposition
11.2
we find to leading order in
\ell
and
n
that

\displaystyle S_{(11)}^{(\ell+1)}

\displaystyle=\chi_{\perp}^{(\ell)}S_{(11)}^{(\ell)}+\frac{C_{W}}{2}K_{(11)}^{(\ell)}S_{(00)}^{(\ell)}\left\langle\partial^{2}(\sigma^{\prime})^{2}\right\rangle_{K^{(\ell)}},

and hence by (
11.9
) we obtain

S_{(11)}^{(\ell)}=\frac{C_{W}e^{-\gamma}}{3nn_{0}}\left(1+O(\ell^{-1})\right)+O(n^{-2})=\left[\frac{\ell}{3n}+O(n^{-1})+O(n^{-2})\right]K_{(11)}^{(\ell)}.

Combining this with (
11.9
) completes our derivation of (
11.29
). A very similar computation reveals

\displaystyle S_{(12)}^{(\ell+1)}

\displaystyle=\chi_{\perp}^{(\ell)}S_{(12)}^{(\ell)}+C_{W}\kappa_{(10)}^{(\ell)}\kappa_{(20)}^{(\ell)}\left[\left\langle\partial^{2}(\sigma^{\prime})^{2}\right\rangle_{K^{(\ell)}}+\frac{1}{2}S_{(00)}^{(\ell)}\left\langle\partial^{4}(\sigma^{\prime})^{2}\right\rangle_{K^{(\ell)}}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{C_{W}}{8}\left\{\kappa_{(00)(00)}^{(\ell)}\left[K_{(12)}^{(\ell)}\left\langle\partial^{4}(\sigma^{\prime})^{2}\right\rangle_{K^{(\ell)}}+K_{(10)}^{(\ell)}K_{(20)}^{(\ell)}\left\langle\partial^{6}(\sigma^{\prime})^{2}\right\rangle_{K^{(\ell)}}\right]\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+2\left(\kappa_{(10)(00)}^{(\ell)}K_{(20)}^{(\ell)}+\kappa_{(20)(00)}^{(\ell)}K_{(10)}^{(\ell)}\right)\left\langle\partial^{4}(\sigma^{\prime})^{2}\right\rangle_{K^{(\ell)}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.+2\left(2\kappa_{(12)(00)}^{(\ell)}+\kappa_{(10)(20)}^{(\ell)}\right)\left\langle\partial^{2}(\sigma^{\prime})^{2}\right\rangle_{K^{(\ell)}}\right\}+O(n^{-2}).

Using the asymptotics from Proposition
11.2
and Lemma
6.2
we obtain

S_{(12)}^{(\ell)}=O\left(\frac{x_{1;\alpha}x_{2;\alpha}}{nn_{0}^{2}\ell}\right)+O(n^{-2}).

Combined with (
11.10
) this proves (
11.30
).
∎

Appendix A
Proof of Theorem
2.2

Our proof of Theorem
2.2
closely follows the proof of Theorem 1.2 in
[
18
]
. Let us first recall the notation and assumptions. We fix
\sigma:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that

•

There exists
r\geq 1
so that the
r
-th derivative of
\sigma
belongs to
L^{\infty}
.

•

There exist
c,c^{\prime}>0
so that

\left|\left|e^{-cx^{2-c^{\prime}}}\frac{d^{r}}{dx^{r}}\sigma(x)\right|\right|_{L^{\infty}}<\infty.

We take
C_{b}\geq
and
C_{W}>0
and consider a random depth
L
neural network with input dimension
n_{0}
, output dimension
n_{L+1}
, hidden layer widths satisfying

\exists c,C>0\text{ s.t. }cn\leq n_{1},\ldots,n_{L}\leq Cn,\qquad n\gg 1,

non-linearity
\sigma
and random weights and biases as in (
2.2
). We also fix a finite collection

x_{\mathcal{A}}:=\left\{x_{\alpha},\quad\alpha\in\mathcal{A}\right\}

of distinct network inputs as well as an integer
m
and study for each
\ell
the random vectors

D^{\leq r}z_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\ell)}:=\left(D^{\leq r}z_{i;\mathcal{A}},\quad i=1,\ldots,m\right),

where

D^{\leq r}z_{i;\mathcal{A}}:=\left(D_{\alpha}^{J}z_{i;\alpha}^{(\ell)},\quad\alpha\in\mathcal{A},\,i=1,\ldots,m,\left|J\right|\leq r\right)

are the derivatives of
z_{i;\mathcal{A}}^{{}_{(\ell)}}
of order at most
r
. Our goal is to show that, as
n\rightarrow\infty
, the joint distribution of the random vectors
D^{\leq r}z_{i;\mathcal{A}}^{{}_{(\ell)}}
converges to that of of centered jointly Gaussian vectors that are independent for different
i
and satisfy

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{Cov}\left(D_{\alpha_{1}}^{J_{1}}z_{i;\alpha_{1}}^{(\ell)},\,D_{\alpha_{2}}^{J_{2}}z_{i;\alpha_{2}}^{(\ell)}\right)=D_{\alpha_{1}}^{J_{1}}D_{\alpha_{2}}^{J_{2}}K_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{(\ell)},

where

K_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{(\ell+1)}=C_{b}+C_{W}\left\langle\sigma(z_{\alpha})\sigma(z_{\beta})\right\rangle_{K^{(\ell)}},\quad K_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{(1)}=C_{b}+C_{W}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}x_{j;\alpha_{1}}x_{j;\alpha_{2}}

is the infinite width covariance from Theorem
2.2
. To prove this, let us denote by
\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}
the sigma algebra generated by the weigts and biases in layer up to and including
\ell
. Observe that, conditional on
\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}
, we have that
D^{\leq r}z_{i;\mathcal{A}}^{{}_{(\ell)}}
are already independent for different
i
and that, since the weights and biases are Gaussian, each is a centered Gaussian with conditional covariance

\mathrm{Cov}\left(D_{\alpha_{1}}^{J_{1}}z_{i;\alpha_{1}}^{(\ell+1)},\,D_{\alpha_{2}}^{J_{2}}z_{i;\alpha_{2}}^{(\ell+1)}~\bigg|~\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}\right)=D_{\alpha_{1}}^{J_{1}}D_{\alpha_{2}}^{J_{2}}\Sigma_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{(\ell)},

where

\Sigma_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{(\ell)}=C_{b}+\frac{C_{W}}{n_{\ell}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{\ell}}\sigma\left(z_{j;\alpha_{1}}^{(\ell)}\right)\sigma\left(z_{j;\alpha_{2}}^{(\ell)}\right).

Thus, by the continuous mapping theorem, it suffices to show that for any multi-indices
J_{1},J_{2}
with
\left|J_{i}\right|\leq r
and any
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathcal{A}
we have

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[D_{\alpha_{1}}^{J_{1}}D_{\alpha_{2}}^{J_{2}}\Sigma_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{(\ell)}\right]\quad\text{exists and is finite}

(A.1)

and

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{Var}\left[D_{\alpha_{1}}^{J_{1}}D_{\alpha_{2}}^{J_{2}}\Sigma_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{(\ell)}\right]=0.

(A.2)

We establish (
A.1
) and (
A.2
) by induction on
\ell
the following more general statement.

Proposition A.1
.

Denote by
N(n_{0},r)
the number of derivatives of order at most
r
in
n_{0}
variables. Consider any measureable function
f:\mathbb{R}^{N(n_{0},r)\times\left|\mathcal{A}\right|}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
that is polynomially bounded, and define

\mathcal{O}_{f}^{(\ell)}:=\frac{1}{n_{\ell}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{\ell}}f\left(D^{\leq r}z_{j;\mathcal{A}}\right).

Then,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{O}_{f}^{(\ell)}\right]\quad\text{exists and is finite}

(A.3)

and

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{Var}\left[\mathcal{O}_{f}^{(\ell)}\right]=0.

(A.4)

Proof.

We proceed by induction, starting with
\ell=1
. Since weights and biases are Gaussian, the vectors
D^{\leq r}z_{i;\mathcal{A}}^{(1)}
are independent for all
i
and jointly Gaussian. The polynomial growth assumption on
f
show the moments of
f(x)
are finite if
x
is Gaussian. This allows us to apply the SLLN to conclude both (
A.3
) and (
A.4
).

Let us now assume we have proved (
A.1
) and (
A.2
) for layers
1,\ldots,\ell
. We start by fixing any polynomially bounded
f
and establishing (
A.1
) at layer
\ell+1.
We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{O}_{f}^{(\ell+1)}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(D^{\leq r}z_{1;\mathcal{A}}\right)\right].

As above, conditionl on
\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}
, we have the following equality in distribution:

D^{\leq r}z_{1;\mathcal{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\left(\Sigma^{\leq r,(\ell)}\right)^{1/2}Z,\qquad Z\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,I_{N(n_{0},r)\times\mathcal{A}}\right)

(A.5)

where
Z
is independent of the conditional covariance matrix

\Sigma^{\leq r,(\ell)}=\left(D_{\alpha_{1}}^{J_{1}}D_{\alpha_{2}}^{J_{2}}\Sigma_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{(\ell)}\right)_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathcal{A}}.

The key observation is that each entry of
\Sigma^{\leq r,(\ell)}
is of the form
\mathcal{O}_{f}^{(\ell)}
for polynomially bounded
f
. Hence, we may apply the inductive hypothesis to conclude that there exists a matrix
\overline{\Sigma}^{{}_{\leq r,(\ell)}}
such that the following convergence in distribution holds

\Sigma^{\leq r,(\ell)}\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\quad\overline{\Sigma}^{\leq r,(\ell)}\qquad\text{ as }\quad n\rightarrow\infty.

The polynomial growth assumption on
f
together with the Skorohod representation theorem and dominated convergence show that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(D^{\leq r}z_{1;\mathcal{A}}\right)\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(\left(\overline{\Sigma}^{\leq r,(\ell)}\right)^{1/2}Z\right)\right]=:\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{f}^{(\ell+1)}\quad\text{exists and is finite}.

This proves (
A.3
). To show (
A.4
), we proceed similarly. Namely, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}\left[\mathcal{O}_{f}^{(\ell+1)}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{\ell+1}}\mathrm{Var}\left[f\left(D^{\leq r}z_{1;\mathcal{A}}^{(\ell+1)}\right)\right]+\left(1-\frac{1}{n_{\ell+1}}\right)\mathrm{Cov}\left(f\left(D^{\leq r}z_{1;\mathcal{A}}^{(\ell+1)}\right),\,f\left(D^{\leq r}z_{2;\mathcal{A}}^{(\ell+1)}\right)\right).

Note that

\mathrm{Var}\left[f\left(D^{\leq r}z_{1;\mathcal{A}}^{(\ell+1)}\right)\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{\ell}}\left[f\left(D^{\leq r}z_{1;\mathcal{A}}^{(\ell+1)}\right)\right]^{2}\right].

Hence, since
f^{2}
is also polynomially bounded we have already shown that (
A.3
) holds at layer
\ell+1
, we see that

\mathrm{Var}\left[\mathcal{O}_{f}^{(\ell+1)}\right]=\mathrm{Cov}\left(f\left(D^{\leq r}z_{1;\mathcal{A}}^{(\ell+1)}\right),\,f\left(D^{\leq r}z_{2;\mathcal{A}}^{(\ell+1)}\right)\right)+O(n^{-1}).

Next, using that conditional on
\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}
the vectors
D^{\leq r}z_{i;\mathcal{A}}^{(\ell+1)}
are independent for different
i
we conclude from the law of total covariance that

\mathrm{Cov}\left(f\left(D^{\leq r}z_{1;\mathcal{A}}^{(\ell+1)}\right),\,f\left(D^{\leq r}z_{2;\mathcal{A}}^{(\ell+1)}\right)\right)\leq\mathrm{Var}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(D^{\leq r}z_{1;\mathcal{A}}^{(\ell+1)}\right)~\bigg|~\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}\right]\right].

Combining the equality in distribution (
A.5
) with the polynomial growth condition on
f
and the dominated convergence theorem we find

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{Var}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(D^{\leq r}z_{1;\mathcal{A}}^{(\ell+1)}\right)~\bigg|~\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}\right]\right]=\mathrm{Var}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(\left(\overline{\Sigma}^{\leq r,(\ell)}\right)^{1/2}Z\right)\right]\right]=0.

This completes the proof that (
A.4
) holds at infinite width, establishing Proposition
A.1
.

∎

Appendix B
Infinite Width Analysis of Tanh-like Non-linearities

The purpose of this section is to derive some basic properties of the infinite width variance recursion

\kappa_{\alpha\alpha}^{(\ell+1)}=C_{b}+C_{W}\left\langle\sigma(z)^{2}\right\rangle_{\kappa_{\alpha\alpha}^{(\ell)}}.

(B.1)

We abbreviate

\sigma_{j}:=\frac{1}{j!}\frac{d^{j}}{dx^{j}}\bigg|_{x=0}\sigma(x)

and consider here the case when
\sigma
that is a tanh-like non-linearity in the sense that
\sigma
satisfies:

•

\sigma
is smooth at
0
with
\sigma_{1}\neq 0

•

\sigma
has the opposite sign as its second derivative

\text{for almost every }z,~\mathrm{sgn}\left(\sigma(z)\sigma^{\prime\prime}(z)\right)=-1.

(B.2)

Note that this forces
\sigma_{2}=0
and

a:=-\frac{6\sigma_{3}}{\sigma_{1}}>0.

•

\sigma
is sub-linear:

\exists C>0\,\text{ s.t. }\forall z\in\mathbb{R}\,\left|\sigma(z)\right|\leq\left|\sigma_{1}z\right|,

(B.3)

•

\sigma
is controlled by its first few non-zero Taylor series coefficients at
0
:

\exists C\geq 0\text{ s.t. }\forall z\geq 0,\quad\sigma_{1}z+\sigma_{3}z^{3}\leq\sigma(z)\leq\sigma_{1}z+\sigma_{3}z^{3}+Cz^{4}

(B.4)

We will be interested in understanding the recursion (
B.1
) at criticality in the sense defined in §
4.1
. Specifically, we remind the reader that this means we choose
C_{b},C_{W}
so that

\displaystyle\exists K_{*}\geq 0\quad\text{ s.t. }\quad K_{*}

\displaystyle=C_{b}+C_{W}\left\langle\sigma^{2}(z)\right\rangle_{K_{*}}

\displaystyle\chi_{||}(K_{*})

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{W}}{2}\left\langle\partial^{2}(\sigma(z)^{2})\right\rangle_{K_{*}}=1

\displaystyle\chi_{\perp}(K_{*})

\displaystyle=C_{W}\left\langle(\sigma^{\prime}(z))^{2}\right\rangle_{K_{*}}=1.

Before stating our main result (Proposition
B.1
), let us explain intuitively what we expect. First of all, as we shall see in Proposition
B.1
,
\tanh
-like non-linearities requires
K_{*}=0
for criticality. Second, by Taylor expanding the recursion (
2.4
) around small values of
K_{\alpha\alpha}^{(\ell)}
we find

\displaystyle K_{\alpha\alpha}^{(\ell+1)}

\displaystyle=K_{\alpha\alpha}^{(\ell)}-a\left(K_{\alpha\alpha}^{(\ell)}\right)^{2}+O\left(\left(K_{\alpha\alpha}^{(\ell)}\right)^{3}\right).

This is well-approximated by the ODE

\frac{d}{dt}K(t)=-aK(t)^{2},

whose solution is

K(t)=\left(at+\frac{1}{K(0)}\right)^{-1}.

This form for the solution has two important properties that we will check in Proposition
B.1
hold for the actual solution
K_{\alpha\alpha}^{(\ell)}
to the discrete difference equation (
2.4
):

•

At large
t
,
K(t)
tends to zero like
1/at
plus an error of size roughly
O(t^{-2})
.

•

The leading order behavior of
K(t)
at large
t
is independent of the initial condition.

Proposition B.1
.

If
\sigma
is a tanh-like non-linearity in the sense defined above then criticality is achieved for
\sigma
only with

K_{*}=0,\qquad C_{b}=0,\qquad\text{and}\qquad C_{W}=\sigma_{1}^{-2}.

(B.5)

Moreover, for every
\delta\in(0,1)
we have

K_{\alpha\alpha}^{(0)}>0\quad\Rightarrow\quad\sup_{\ell\geq 1}\ell^{2-\delta}\left|K_{\alpha\alpha}^{(\ell)}-\frac{1}{a\ell}\right|<\infty.

(B.6)

Proof.

Note that for any
K\geq 0
we have

\chi_{||}(K)=\chi_{\perp}(K)+C_{W}\left\langle\sigma(z)\sigma^{\prime\prime}(z)\right\rangle_{K}.

(B.7)

Hence, at criticality, we must have

\left\langle\sigma(z)\sigma^{\prime\prime}(z)\right\rangle_{K_{*}}=0.

But due to assumption (
B.2
) we have

K>0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\left\langle\sigma(z)\sigma^{\prime\prime}(z)\right\rangle_{K}<0.

Thus, we indeed find that we must have
K_{*}=0
at criticality. Hence, in light of (
B.7
) criticality is equivalent to the system of equations

K_{*}=0=C_{b}+C_{W}\sigma(0)^{2},\qquad\chi_{||}(0)=\chi_{\perp}(0)=C_{W}\left\langle(\sigma^{\prime}(z))^{2}\right\rangle_{0}=C_{W}\sigma_{1}^{2}=1.

This system has a unique solution:

C_{b}=0,\quad C_{W}=\sigma_{1}^{-2},

completing the proof of the criticality conditions (
B.5
). Let us now establish (
B.6
). First note that at criticality the sub-linearity condition (
B.3
) guarantees that for all
\delta>0
there exists
c_{\delta}\in(0,1)
such that

K>\delta\quad\Longrightarrow\quad C_{W}\left\langle\sigma(z)^{2}\right\rangle_{K}<(1-c_{\delta})\left\langle z^{2}\right\rangle_{K}=(1-c_{\delta})K.

Hence, for all
K,\delta>0
there exists
\ell_{0}\geq 1
such that

K_{\alpha\alpha}^{(0)}\leq K\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad K_{\alpha\alpha}^{(\ell)}\leq\delta\quad\forall\ell\geq\ell_{0}.

(B.8)

In particular,
K_{\alpha\alpha}^{{}_{(\ell)}}
is monotonically decreasing and converges to
K_{*}=0
as
\ell
grows. Let us now define for each
\ell\geq 1

K_{\alpha\alpha}^{(\ell)}=:\frac{1}{a\ell}+\epsilon^{(\ell)},\qquad a:=-6\frac{\sigma_{3}}{\sigma_{1}}>0,

where
a
is positive due to (
B.2
). Note that since
K_{\alpha\alpha}^{(\ell)}
tends to zero with
\ell
, so does
\epsilon^{(\ell)}
. Let us agree that for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
the symbol
t^{+}
(resp.
t^{-}
) means that for
\ell
sufficiently large we may make the constant
t^{+}
(resp.
t^{-}
) arbitrary close to
t
from above (resp. below). In order to prove (
B.6
), we start with the following elementary estimate.

Lemma B.2
.

For all
\ell\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\epsilon^{(\ell+1)}~\geq~-\frac{1}{a\ell^{2}(\ell+1)}+\epsilon^{(\ell)}\left(1-\frac{2}{\ell}-a\epsilon^{(\ell)}\right).

(B.9)

Further, there exists a constant
C>0
depending only on
\sigma
with the following property. For all
K>0
there exists a constant
\ell_{0}\geq 1
so that if
K_{\alpha\alpha}^{{}_{(0)}}\leq K
, then for all
\delta\in(0,1)
we have

\displaystyle\epsilon^{(\ell+1)}~\leq~\frac{C}{\ell^{3}}+\epsilon^{(\ell)}\left(1-\frac{2-\delta}{\ell}\right),\qquad\forall~\ell\geq\ell_{\delta}:=\max\left\{\frac{C}{\delta},\,\frac{2C}{a},\,\ell_{0}\right\}.

(B.10)

Proof.

Plugging in the estimates (
B.4
) into the recursion (
B.1
) yields for some
C>0
depending only on
\sigma

\displaystyle\epsilon^{(\ell+1)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\ell^{3}}+\epsilon^{(\ell)}\left[1-\frac{2}{\ell}+\frac{C}{\ell^{2}}\right]+\left(\epsilon^{(\ell)}\right)^{2}\left(-a+\frac{C}{\ell}\right)+C(\epsilon^{(\ell)})^{3}

Note that for all
\ell\geq 2C/a
we have
-a+C/\ell\leq 0
. Hence, for all
\ell\geq\max\left\{2C/a,C/\delta\right\}
we have

\displaystyle\epsilon^{(\ell+1)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\ell^{3}}+\epsilon^{(\ell)}\left[1-\frac{2-\delta}{\ell}\right]+C(\epsilon^{(\ell)})^{3}.

Moreover, if
\epsilon^{(\ell)}\leq 0
, then
(\epsilon^{(\ell)})^{3}\leq 0
. If on the other hand
\epsilon^{(\ell)}\geq 0
, then from (
B.8
) we find that there for all
K>0
there exists
\ell_{0}
so that
(\epsilon^{(\ell)})^{3}\leq a(\epsilon^{(\ell)})^{2}/4
for all
\ell\geq\ell_{0}.
Hence, in all cases, for each
\delta\in(0,1)
if
\ell\geq\max\left\{2C/a,C/\delta,\ell_{0}\right\},
we find

\displaystyle\epsilon^{(\ell+1)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\ell^{3}}+\epsilon^{(\ell)}\left[1-\frac{2-\delta}{\ell}\right],

as claimed. The lower bound follows from a similar but simpler computation.
∎

Fix
\delta\in(0,1)
. The relation (
B.10
), together with Lemma
6.2
, show that for all
K>0
there exists some
C^{\prime}>0
depending on
\delta,\sigma,K
such that if
K_{\alpha\alpha}^{{}_{(0)}}\leq K
then

\epsilon^{(\ell+1)}\leq\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=\ell_{\delta}}^{\ell}\frac{C}{\ell^{3}}\prod_{\ell^{\prime\prime}=\ell^{\prime}+1}^{\ell}\left(1-\frac{2^{-}}{\ell}\right)\leq C^{\prime}\left[\frac{1}{\ell^{2}}+\epsilon^{(\ell_{\delta})}\frac{1}{\ell^{2-\delta}}\right].

This shows that

\forall\delta\in(0,1)~\exists\ell_{\delta}\geq 1~\text{ s.t. }\quad\epsilon^{(\ell)}\leq\frac{1}{\ell^{2-\delta}}\quad\forall\ell\geq\ell_{\delta}.

(B.11)

To conclude (
B.6
) it therefore remains to deduce that

\forall K_{1},K_{2}>0,\,\delta\in(0,1)~\exists\ell_{\delta}\geq 1~\text{ s.t. }\quad K_{1}<K_{\alpha\alpha}^{(0)}<K_{2}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\epsilon^{(\ell)}\geq-\frac{1}{\ell^{2-\delta}}\quad\forall\ell\geq\ell_{\delta}.

(B.12)

To aid with this, we will need the following

Lemma B.3
.

For any
\delta\in(0,1)
there exists
\ell_{\delta}\geq 1
with the property that if
\ell\geq\ell_{\delta}
then

\epsilon^{(\ell)}\geq-\ell^{-2+\delta}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\epsilon^{(\ell+1)}\geq-(\ell+1)^{-2+\delta}.

Proof.

Suppose
\epsilon^{(\ell)}\geq-\ell^{-2+\delta}
. The lower bound in (
B.9
) yields for some
C,C^{\prime}>0

\displaystyle\epsilon^{(\ell+1)}+\left(\ell+1\right)^{-2+\delta}

\displaystyle\geq\left(\ell+1\right)^{-2+\delta}\left[1-\left(1+\ell^{-1}\right)^{2-\delta}\right]-2\ell^{-3+\delta}-C\ell^{-4+2\delta}

\displaystyle\geq\delta\ell^{-3+\delta}-C^{\prime}(\ell^{-3}+\ell^{-4+2\delta}),

which is non-negative for all
\ell
sufficiently large.
∎

We are now in a position to establish (
B.12
). In light of the previous Lemma we need only consider the case when

\forall\delta\in(0,1)~\exists~\ell_{\delta}\geq 1\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\epsilon^{(\ell_{\delta})}<-\ell^{2-\delta}.

Note that in light of the upper bound (
B.11
) we find that for all
\delta\in(0,1)
there exists
\ell_{\delta}\geq 1
and
C_{\delta}>0
so that for all
\ell\geq\ell_{\delta}
we have

\displaystyle K_{\alpha\alpha}^{(\ell+1)}\geq K_{\alpha\alpha}^{(\ell)}\left(1-aK_{\alpha\alpha}^{(\ell)}\right)\geq K_{\alpha\alpha}^{(\ell)}\left(1-a\left(-\frac{1}{a\ell}+C_{\delta}\ell^{-2+\delta}\right)\right)=K_{\alpha\alpha}^{(\ell)}\left(1-\frac{1}{\ell}-aC_{\delta}\ell^{-2+\delta}\right).

Hence, assuming
K_{2}\geq K_{\alpha\alpha}^{(0)}\geq K_{1}>0,
we may iterate this inequality to find that there exists
c>0
depending on
K_{1},K_{2}
and
\ell_{0}\geq 1
so that

K_{\alpha\alpha}^{(\ell)}\geq\frac{c}{a\ell}\qquad\forall\ell\geq\ell_{0}.

Hence, since
\epsilon^{(\ell)}<0
for all
\ell\geq\ell_{\delta}
we find for all
\ell\geq\max\left\{\ell_{0},\ell_{\delta}\right\}
that

-a(\epsilon^{(\ell)})^{2}\geq\epsilon^{(\ell)}\frac{1-c}{\ell}

Substituting this into the lower bound (
B.9
), we find that for all
\ell\geq\max\left\{\ell_{0},\ell_{\delta}\right\}

\epsilon^{(\ell+1)}\geq-\frac{C^{\prime}}{\ell^{3}}+\epsilon^{(\ell)}\left(1-\frac{1+c}{\ell}\right).

Since
\epsilon^{(\ell_{\delta})}<0
, we see by applying Lemma
6.2
that there exists
C>0
so that for all
\ell\geq\max\left\{\ell_{0},\ell_{\delta}\right\}

\epsilon^{(\ell+1)}\geq-\frac{C}{\ell^{1+c}}.

But now we can bootstrap this estimate. Indeed, for any
\delta\in(0,1)
we substitute this into the lower bound (
B.9
) to find that for all
\ell
sufficiently large

\epsilon^{(\ell+1)}\geq-\frac{C^{\prime}}{\ell^{3}}+\epsilon^{(\ell)}\left(1-\frac{2-\delta}{\ell}\right).

Again applying Lemma
6.2
yields that for all
\ell
sufficiently large

\epsilon^{(\ell+1)}\geq-\frac{C}{\ell^{2-\delta}}.

This completes the proof.

∎

Appendix C
Exact Solutions for
1
-homogeneous activations

In this appendix, we collect several known computations related to the distribution of neuron activations in random fully connected networks with
1-
homogeneous activations. Specifically, we fix a one homogeneous non-linearity

\sigma(t)=(a_{+}{\bf 1}_{t>0}+a_{-}{\bf 1}_{t<0})t

and consider a random fully connected neural network with input dimension
n_{0}
, output dimension
n_{L+1}
, hidden layer widths
n_{1},\ldots,n_{\ell}
, and non-linearity
\sigma
that is tuned to criticality in the sense that

C_{b}=0,\qquad C_{W}=\frac{2}{a_{+}^{2}+a_{-}^{2}}.

Our first task is to derive in §
C.1
a known exact formula for the infinite width covariance
K_{\alpha\beta}^{(\ell+1)}
as a function of
K_{\alpha\alpha}^{(\ell)},K_{\alpha\beta}^{(\ell)},K_{\beta\beta}^{(\ell)}
. Then, in Section §
C.2
, we sketch a derivation of the limiting distribution (
4.7
) of a neuron pre-activation in the double scaling limit
n,L\rightarrow\infty,L/n\rightarrow\gamma
.

C.1
Covariance Propagation in Random Fulluy Connected
1
-homogeneous Networks

In this section, we consider two network inputs
x_{\alpha},x_{\beta}
of the same norm:

K_{\alpha\alpha}^{(0)}=\frac{1}{n_{0}}\left|\left|x_{\alpha}\right|\right|^{2}=K=\frac{1}{n_{0}}\left|\left|x_{\beta}\right|\right|^{2}=K_{\beta\beta}^{(0)},\qquad K>0.

(C.1)

Let us define

\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}^{(\ell)}:=\frac{1-\mathrm{Corr}_{\alpha\beta}^{(\ell)}}{2},\qquad\mathrm{Corr}_{\alpha\beta}^{(\ell)}:=\frac{K_{\alpha\beta}^{(\ell)}}{\left(K_{\alpha\alpha}^{(\ell)}K_{\beta\beta}^{(\ell)}\right)^{1/2}}

Our goal is to derive the following explicit recursion for
\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}^{(\ell+1)}
in terms of
\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}^{(\ell)}
. This derivation follows the approach in §5.5
[
53
]
. To the author’s knowledge, the following formula (or really something equivalent) was first derived in
[
cho2009kernel
]
.

Proposition C.1

(Correlation propagation for
1-
homogeneous activation functions)
.

At criticality, we have the following exact formula:

\displaystyle 1-2\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}^{(\ell+1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2C_{W}(a_{+}-a_{-})^{2}}{\pi}\left[\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}^{(\ell)}(1-\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}^{(\ell)})}+\left(\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}^{(\ell)}\right)\cos^{-1}\left(\sqrt{\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}^{(\ell)}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+C_{W}a_{+}a_{-}(1-2\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}^{(\ell)})

(C.2)

In particular, taking
\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta}^{(\ell)}
small we find

\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta}^{(\ell+1)}=\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta}^{(\ell)}-\frac{4}{3\pi}\left(\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta}^{(\ell)}\right)^{3/2}+O\left(\left(\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta}^{(\ell)}\right)^{5/2}\right).

Hence, as
\ell\rightarrow\infty
,

\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta}^{(\ell)}=\frac{2}{3\pi}\ell^{-2}(1+o(1)).

Proof.

We have from Theorem
2.2
that

K_{\alpha\beta}^{(\ell+1)}=C_{b}+C_{W}\left\langle\sigma(z_{\alpha})\sigma(z_{\beta})\right\rangle_{K^{(\ell)}},

(C.3)

where we recall that the brackets above mean the average with respect to the Gaussian distribution

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}z_{\alpha}\\
z_{\beta}\end{array}\right)~\sim~\mathcal{N}\left(0,\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}K_{\alpha\alpha}^{(\ell)}&K_{\alpha\beta}^{(\ell)}\\
K_{\alpha\beta}^{(\ell)}&K_{\beta\beta}^{(\ell)}\end{array}\right)\right).

Since we are at criticality, we have

C_{b}=0,\qquad C_{W}=\frac{2}{a_{+}^{2}+a_{-}^{2}}

and that moreover

K_{\alpha\alpha}^{(\ell)}=K_{\beta\beta}^{(\ell)}=K,

where
K
is the constant from (
C.1
). Our first step is to change from the Gaussian variables
z_{\alpha},z_{\beta}
to the new Gaussian variables

\xi=\frac{z_{\alpha}+z_{\beta}}{2\sqrt{K}},\qquad\eta=\frac{z_{\alpha}-z_{\beta}}{2\sqrt{K}}.

We have

z_{\alpha}=\sqrt{K}(\xi+\eta),\qquad z_{\beta}=\sqrt{K}\left(\xi-\eta\right).

Moreover, writing

\epsilon:=\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}^{(\ell)}=\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{K_{\alpha\beta}^{(\ell)}}{\left(K_{\alpha\alpha}^{(\ell)}K_{\beta\beta}^{(\ell)}\right)^{1/2}}\right)

we find

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}[\xi]=1-\varepsilon,\qquad\mathrm{Var}[\eta]=\varepsilon,\qquad\mathrm{Cov}[\xi,\eta]=0.

Hence, the right hand side of the recursion (
C.3
) reads

C_{W}K\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sigma\left((1-\varepsilon)^{1/2}\xi+\varepsilon^{1/2}\eta\right)\sigma\left((1-\varepsilon)^{1/2}\xi-\varepsilon^{1/2}\eta\right)\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\left(\xi^{2}+\eta^{2}\right)\right]\frac{d\xi d\eta}{2\pi}.

Using the definition of
\sigma
yields

\displaystyle\sigma\left((1-\varepsilon)^{1/2}\xi+\varepsilon^{1/2}\eta\right)\sigma\left((1-\varepsilon)^{1/2}\xi-\varepsilon^{1/2}\eta\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\left(a_{+}{\bf 1}_{\xi+\eta>0}+a_{-}{\bf 1}_{\xi+\eta<0}\right)\left(a_{+}{\bf 1}_{\xi-\eta>0}+a_{-}{\bf 1}_{\xi-\eta<0}\right)((1-\epsilon)\xi^{2}-\epsilon\eta^{2}).

Changing variables
(\xi,\eta)\rightarrow(-\xi,-\eta)
inside the integral and averaging yields

\displaystyle K_{\alpha\beta}^{(\ell+1)}

\displaystyle=C_{W}Ka_{+}a_{-}(1-2\epsilon)

\displaystyle+\frac{C_{W}K(a_{+}-a_{-})^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}{\bf 1}_{(1-\epsilon)\xi^{2}-\epsilon\eta^{2}>0}((1-\epsilon)\xi^{2}-\epsilon\eta^{2})\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\left(\xi^{2}+\eta^{2}\right)\right]\frac{d\xi d\eta}{2\pi}.

Passing to polar coordinates and explicitly computing the resulting integral is now straightforward and completes the derivation of (
C.2
).
∎

C.2
Full Distribution of Neuron Pre-activations at a Single Input and the Derivation of (
4.7
)

Our purpose in this section is to briefly recall an exact formula for the full distribution of a neuron pre-activation
z_{i;\alpha}^{(L+1)}
. For this, note that since
x_{\alpha}\mapsto z_{\alpha}^{(L+1)}
is piecewise linear and the event that the Jacobian
J_{x_{\alpha}}z_{\alpha}^{(L+1)}
is not well-defined at
x_{\alpha}
has probability zero, we may write

z_{\alpha}^{(L+1)}=J_{x_{\alpha}}z_{\alpha}^{(L+1)}x_{\alpha}.

Next,

J_{x_{\alpha}}z_{\alpha}^{(L+1)}=W^{(L+1)}D^{(L)}W^{(L)}\cdots D^{(1)}W^{(1)},

(C.4)

where
W^{(\ell)}
are simply the weight matrices and

D^{(\ell)}:=\mathrm{Diag}\left(\sigma^{\prime}(z_{i;\alpha}^{(\ell)}),\,i=1,\ldots,n_{\ell}\right).

Arguing exactly as in Proposition 2 of
[
21
]
, we have the following equality in distribution:

D^{(L)}W^{(L)}\cdots D^{(1)}W^{(1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}A\widehat{D}^{(L)}W^{(L)}\cdots\widehat{D}^{(1)}W^{(1)},

where
A
is a diagonal matrix with iid
\pm 1
entries on the diagonal that is independent of
W^{(\ell)}
and the diagonal matrices

\widehat{D}^{(\ell)}=\mathrm{Diag}\bigg(\underbrace{a_{+}\xi_{i}^{(\ell)}+a_{-}(1-\xi_{i}^{(\ell)})}_{=:d_{i}^{(\ell)}},\,i=1,\ldots,n_{\ell}\bigg),\qquad\xi_{i}^{(\ell)}\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(1/2)\,\,iid.

Combining this with (
C.4
) and recalling that the entries of
W^{(L+1)}
are iid centered Gaussians with variance
C_{W}/n_{L}
yields

z_{i;\alpha}^{(L+1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}Z_{1}\cdot\left(\frac{C_{W}}{n_{L}}\right)^{1/2}\left|\left|\widehat{D}^{(L)}W^{(L)}\cdots\widehat{D}^{(1)}W^{(1)}x_{\alpha}\right|\right|,

where
Z_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
is independent of
\widehat{D}^{(\ell)},W^{(\ell)},\,i=1,\ldots,L
. Further, due to the right orthogonal invariance of the Gaussian matrices
W^{(\ell)}
and the normalization that the variance of the entries of
W^{(\ell)}
is
C_{W}/n_{\ell-11}
, we have that

\displaystyle\log\left[\left(\frac{C_{W}}{n_{L}}\right)^{1/2}\left|\left|\widehat{D}^{(L)}W^{(L)}\cdots\widehat{D}^{(1)}W^{(1)}x_{\alpha}\right|\right|\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{1}{2}\log\left[\frac{C_{W}}{n_{0}}\left|\left|x_{\alpha}\right|\right|^{2}\right]+\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\frac{1}{2}\log\left[\frac{C_{W}}{n_{L}}\left|\left|\widehat{D}^{(\ell)}\widehat{W}^{(\ell)}u^{(\ell)}\right|\right|^{2}\right]

where
u^{(\ell)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{\ell-1}}
is collection of deterministic unit vectors and
\widehat{W}^{(\ell)}
are independent random matrices with iid standard Gaussian entries. The summands on the previous line are independent and are each distributed like the logarithm of a randomly weighted
\chi^{2}
random viable:

\frac{C_{W}}{n_{\ell}}\left|\left|\widehat{D}^{(\ell)}W^{(\ell)}u^{(\ell)}\right|\right|^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{C_{W}}{n_{\ell}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{\ell}}\left(d_{i}^{(\ell)}\right)^{2}\left(Z_{i}^{(\ell)}\right)^{2},

where
Z_{i}^{(\ell)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
are iid and independent of
d_{i}^{(\ell)}
. Putting this all together, we find that

\displaystyle z_{i;\alpha}^{(L+1)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\left(\frac{C_{W}}{n_{0}}\left|\left|x_{\alpha}\right|\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\cdot Z_{1}\cdot\prod_{\ell=1}^{L}\left(\frac{C_{W}}{n_{\ell}}\right)^{1/2}\left|\left|\widehat{D}^{(\ell)}W^{(\ell)}u^{(\ell)}\right|\right|

is a product of
L+1
independent random variables. Moreover, a direct computation shows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\log\left[\frac{C_{W}}{n_{\ell}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{\ell}}\left(d_{i}^{(\ell)}\right)^{2}\left(Z_{i}^{(\ell)}\right)^{2}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Var}\left[\frac{C_{W}}{n_{\ell}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{\ell}}\left(d_{i}^{(\ell)}\right)^{2}\left(Z_{i}^{(\ell)}\right)^{2}\right]+O(n_{\ell}^{-2})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2n_{\ell}}\left(6\frac{a_{+}^{4}+a_{-}^{4}}{(a_{+}^{2}+a_{-}^{2})^{2}}-1\right)+O(n_{\ell}^{-2})

and also that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}\left[\log\left[\frac{C_{W}}{n_{\ell}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{\ell}}\left(d_{i}^{(\ell)}\right)^{2}\left(Z_{i}^{(\ell)}\right)^{2}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Var}\left[\frac{C_{W}}{n_{\ell}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{\ell}}\left(d_{i}^{(\ell)}\right)^{2}\left(Z_{i}^{(\ell)}\right)^{2}\right]+O(n_{\ell}^{-2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{\ell}}\left(6\frac{a_{+}^{4}+a_{-}^{4}}{(a_{+}^{2}+a_{-}^{2})^{2}}-1\right)+O(n_{\ell}^{-2}).

Combining the preceding two estimates, taking
n,L\rightarrow\infty
with
L/n\rightarrow\gamma
and applying the CLT yields

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}n,L\rightarrow\infty\\
L/n\rightarrow\gamma\in[0,\infty)\end{subarray}}z_{i;\alpha}^{(L)}\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\quad\left(\frac{C_{W}}{n_{0}}\left|\left|x_{\alpha}\right|\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}Z_{1}\exp\left[-\mu(\gamma,a_{+},a_{-})+\sigma(\gamma,a_{+},a_{-})Z_{2}\right],

where

\mu(\gamma,a_{+},a_{-})=\sigma^{2}(\gamma,a_{+},a_{-}):=\frac{\gamma}{4}\left(6\frac{a_{+}^{4}+a_{-}^{4}}{(a_{+}^{2}+a_{+}^{2})^{2}}-1\right),\quad Z_{1},Z_{2}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)\text{ iid}.

This is precisely the statement of (
4.7
).
